It prints nothing to standard output and one fixed line to standard error, so what an overlay's hook prints never reaches the harness; `context` goes to the person's next prompt as the other events' does. The host stays the one place that runs hooks, with every bound and the environment above.

The connection's private directory (`canoa-profile-PID-RANDOM`) is made once, at the first thing written under it, in `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` where that is set and exists (Unix) and the temporary directory otherwise: created non-recursively, never when something is already at the path, with mode `0700` and checked to be a directory of this user's with no access for others (Unix), or with every inherited right removed and only OWNER RIGHTS and the system granted (Windows, through `icacls`, so no name is looked up); a session's directory is made inside it the same way, and the connection's directory is removed only when the connection ends. A directory that cannot be made that way is not used. At the start of a connection, directories of the same kind a dead host left (`canoa-hooks-*` and `canoa-profile-*`, owned by this user, an hour old and, on Unix, whose host (the pid in the name) no longer runs or that is a day old, or, on Windows, that no host holds open) are removed. The channel is a loopback TCP listener per connection, with `127.0.0.1:PORT` and a 128-bit secret for each session request in that request's `channel.json` (never on a command line or in the environment). On Linux only connections from this user's own processes are heard (the socket's owner, as for the delegation tool server); the secrets keep other users and other connections out, and not the person's own sessions from one another, whose agents the person already trusts. A request is one line of at most 8 KiB: `{secret, nonce, kind, fields}`. The host compares the secret against every one it holds in constant time, drops with no answer anything else (a bad secret, a line past its bound, JSON that is not one request, a kind it does not know), queues at most 64 and runs at most 16 handlers, each of which has 250 ms in all to receive its line, and answers `{nonce, block}` with the forwarder's own nonce, which the forwarder checks; a block is honoured only for `pre-compact`. A session loaded again is given the plugin and secret it already has. The reports are read between the messages the host relays (every 50 ms at most), so an event's hooks run on the relay's thread as the other events' do; a secret ends with its session (`session/close`, or the connection) and a report for a session that is not open is dropped. A forwarder that is not answered (no host, an unreadable or missing channel file, an answer not signed by the secret, a refused secret, a timeout of 130 s) stops the harness only when it was given `--refuse`, exit status 2; for everything else it lets the harness go on. A hook that fails with `on_failure = "refuse"` blocks the compaction, as for a prompt.

Per harness and event (measured through the real adapters on Claude Code 2.1.311 and Codex 0.166.0; each cell is a test or a recorded measurement):

| Event | Claude Code (`coqui-acp-claude`, `canoa agent claude`) | Codex (`coqui-acp-codex`) | Gemini CLI, Pi, others |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `cd` | fired, from the working directory after each batch of tool calls | cannot: Codex has no event for it | cannot |
| `pre-compact` | fired for the person's `/compact` (`manual`), through `PreCompact`; `auto` is the same hook and was not triggered | cannot: the adapter has no compaction request, and Codex's hook has no trigger over ACP | cannot |
| `session-start` `compact` | fired, after a `/compact` | cannot, as `pre-compact` | cannot |
| `session-start` `clear` | cannot: the person's `/clear` ends Claude Code's session and the adapter's, so no session starts after it | cannot: no `/clear` over ACP | cannot |

Codex's own hooks could carry these through a `hooks.json` and a trust line (`[hooks.state.'KEY'] trusted_hash`) in its home, but none of the four events is reachable over ACP, so the host writes nothing into a Codex home. The report says each as `hook-unsupported` with the reason.

## AOS overlays (`coqui-aos`)

`canoa_host` has no AOS module of its own: overlays are read, validated, digested, composed and their environment built by the [`coqui-aos`](https://github.com/Boriken-Dev/coqui-aos) crate (a git dependency at one revision; its `AGENTS.md` is the API header), which passes the specification's conformance vectors in full. What is Canoa's own is how a profile uses them (`profile::overlay_layer`, `profile::hosted`), held to `tests/vectors/profile.json`:

- **Reading.** The manifest is `coqui_aos::read_overlay` at the default `Settings`: `OVERLAY.md`'s front matter (restricted YAML, or the format its first line names) or `overlay.cfg` (TOML, JSON after `#!json`, YAML after `#!yaml`); a tag, anchor, alias or merge key is refused, and so are both files together (`manifest` refusal, subject the reader's class). A body of `OVERLAY.md` that is context becomes a `Context::Text` (the front matter is no context), never the file; `CONTEXT.md` beside it is not applied and is listed in `not_applied` as `context: CONTEXT.md, ignored ...`. Mapping order is the document's order; every order Canoa shows is key byte order.
- **A tree** is a `coqui_aos::OverlayTree` (every member's path and bytes), as `coqui_aos::read_tree` reads a directory (`OverlayInput::tree`); `overlay_layer` digests it again with `digest_entries`. A path under an excluded name is not in a tree `read_tree` returns, so it is neither digested nor delivered.
- **Limits** are `coqui_aos::Settings`: an installed overlay at the defaults (4096 files, 64 MiB), a host-profile skill's directory at 1024 files and 16 MiB.
- **The environment** is `coqui_aos::composed_environment` over the overlays in composition order, once after each overlay (`build_each`), from an `Environment` of the process variables an overlay's text names (any word of its manifest or env files), each readable by a placeholder and granted to the `env` provider. `Built::owned` is a variable an overlay set, even to the value the process holds (found by composing again over a base whose values are marked). A refusal is `ProfileError::Resolve` with key `overlay:<identity>.env.<subject>`.
- **The report** (`OverlayReport`) is `coqui_aos::effective` over the manifest and its env files with every variable their text names granted and empty: `non_defaults` are the specification's for what Canoa applies (`unpinned`, `env-passthrough`, `secret-read`, `env-sensitive`, `insecure-transport`; not `setup-network`, `deps-network` and the rest, since nothing of them runs; `env-script` is not listed here either, since the connection's own report lists each script that ran). When that call refuses (a partial lock, an env-path conflict) the report lists none, since the environment would refuse too.
- **A server over plain `http`** to another host (`insecure-transport` in that report) never gets `headers_env`.

## The control channel (`canoa_host::control`)

What belongs to no agent entry: the session ledger, the worktrees and, later, the overlay store. One host-level service,
named `@control` (the `@` is not a name an entry can have), speaking JSON-RPC 2.0, one message to a line as ACP does, but not ACP:
no `initialize`, requests and their answers only. Errors: `-32601` no such method, `-32602` bad params, `-32001` **denied** (the
caller has no grant for it), `-32002` **refused** (a limit), `-32003` not found.

**Who reaches it, and as whom**

- *The app*, in this process: `Local::open("@control")` (`canoa_ffi_link_open` with that entry name). The caller is the **owner**: every
  method, every record. Nothing on a network can obtain a `Local`.
- *A paired client*, from the outside: only when `[host.control]` names an address or pipe (`listen`, as an entry has it; default
  none, so no network control) and lists the key under `grants`. The connection proves its key as an entry's does (same method,
  same pairing); a paired key `grants` does not name is turned away after the proof, as an entry's `peers` turns one away. The
  caller is that **peer**: only the methods its grants name.

```toml
[host.control]
listen = "127.0.0.1:0"                              # or a pipe, as an entry has it; without it there is no network control
grants = { "sha256:…" = ["ledger.read", "ledger.write", "worktree", "store.read", "folders", "files", "config.read", "config.write", "config.write.sessions", "upload", "host.restart", "overlay.read", "overlay.write", "store.programs", "accounts", "discover", "usage", "terminal", "state", "workspace.open", "hosting"] }   # per key; a key not here gets nothing
max_records = 10000                                 # ledger records the host keeps, per host; the oldest by `updated` are refused at, not dropped
max_record_bytes = 262144                           # one ledger record
max_upload_bytes = 8388608                          # one uploaded file, sent in chunks of at most 512 KiB
max_upload_total_bytes = 268435456                  # what one key may keep uploaded
max_uploads = 256                                   # files one key may keep uploaded
```

| Grant | Methods |
| --- | --- |
| `ledger.read` | `ledger/list`, `sessions/running` |
| `ledger.write` | `ledger/put`, `ledger/remove`, `ledger/clear` |
| `worktree` | `worktree/new`, `worktree/status`, `worktree/remove`, `worktree/diff` |
| `store.read` | `store/list`, `store/report`, `store/latest`, `store/plan`, `host/which` |
| `overlay.read` / `store.programs` | `store/index`: the first reads the catalog entries, the second the program entries; a key holding either gets that half, none is refused; `refresh` also needs `overlay.write` |
| `folders` | `fs/list`, `fs/info`, `fs/mentions`, `fs/resolve`, `fs/realpath`, `fs/repo_root`, `fs/guards`, `fs/scratch`, `fs/scratch_remove`, `fs/scratch_preview`, `profile/project` (a folder's `.canoa/profile.toml`, to be shown and trusted) |
| `workspace.open` | `workspace/openers` `{}` -> `{"os", "hostname"?, "openers": [{"id", "label"}]}` (`vscode` only when VS Code is found: Code.exe through the registry's App Paths or its install folders on Windows, the bundle on a Mac, `code` on PATH on Linux; `file_manager`, `reveal`; it starts nothing) and `workspace/open` `{"path", "opener"}` -> `{"started": true}`: starts that program for the folder (`reveal`: a file or folder to select), detached, as this user, through the host's spawn module and never a shell. The path is as `fs/info` takes it (a key not holding `files` keeps to the home and its entries' working roots); a missing VS Code, a path that is no folder or one this key may not see is refused in words. Not in `[host.native]`'s defaults |
| `files` | `fs/read`, and with `folders` an `fs/list` that is not kept to the home and the working roots: this computer's files as the account the host runs as can read them. The grant is the only gate: a key holding it reads and lists whatever that account can, anywhere (the owner, the person's own browser or app, the portal's person on their own host); there is no list of places it is kept to. Not in the browser door's default grants, and a delegated child connection never has it |
| `config.read` | `config/get`, `trust/list`, `looks/list`, `providers/list`, `profile_items/list`, `settings/section_get`, `panel_tabs/list` |
| `config.write` | `config/set` of the preference sections, `trust/revoke`, `settings/section_set` |
| `config.write.sessions` | `config/set` of `overlays`, `loadouts`, `defaults`, `profiles` and `permissions`, which change what an agent may do or is given (the permission mode, protected paths, the overlays, skills and servers every `own_profile` session starts with, a profile's starting instructions): as much as access to the agent; also `trust/set`, which makes a project's profile apply to the sessions in its folder |
| `upload` | `upload/put`, `upload/remove` |
| `host.restart` | `host/restart` |
| `overlay.read` | `overlay/installed`, `overlay/read`, `catalog/read`, `skill/list`, `skill/read`, `profile/canonical`, `profile/tree` (an overlay's folder resolved and read whole, for the app to show a profile's layers), `store/contents`, `store/contents_put` (the key's own cache of what a catalog's entries provide) |
| `overlay.write` | `registry/fetch`, `overlay/check`, `overlay/apply`, `overlay/remove`, `overlay/discard`, `store/read_ahead`, `catalog/fetch`, `catalog/remove`, `skill/fetch`, `skill/scan`, `skill/save`, `skill/remove`, `skill/search`; it can replace an installed overlay that `[overlays]` selects, which changes the context and environment of the next `own_profile` session (hooks and MCP servers stay pinned by digest), so it is as much as access to the agent |
| `store.programs` | `store/apply`, `store/rollback`, `store/remove`, and `store/report` with `probe` or `pick`: install, update, roll back and remove an agent program on the host's programs shelf. It downloads and runs code as the user, and a program it installs is then what an entry may run, so it is as much as access to the agent; `[host.control]` refuses `store.write` as a grant |
| `discover` | `discover/nearby` `{"seconds"?}` (whole seconds, default 3, at most 10) -> `{"candidates": [{label, url, peer, method, source, webtransport, webtransportHash, acpVersions, offers}], "directory"?, "networkError"?}`: the host reads this user's descriptors (agents listening on loopback) and browses `_acp-agent._tcp` for that long (`networkError` says why it could not, the descriptors still being read). Granting it to a key reveals this machine's loopback agents and the link it is on to whoever holds the key, so a network key gets it only by `[host.control] grants`. `directory` is `"shared"` when another user can change the descriptor folder (on Unix: group or other write permission, or an owner who is neither this user nor root) and none of its descriptors were read, and `"unchecked"` when the platform could not show the folder to be this user's alone (Windows, for a folder named by `COQUI_ACP_DESCRIPTORS`; the default under `%LOCALAPPDATA%` is believed) and they were read. A call that arrives while a search runs waits for it and answers what it found, so there is no busy error. Every field is a claim of whatever answered; a portal host does not give the grant to its people. MCP has no service type here: a server shows only as a candidate's `offers` |
| `accounts` | `pools/list` `{}` -> `{"pools": [{name, standard, automatic, accounts}]}` (the person's own in the file's order, then the host's `any-<standard>`, which are `automatic` and in no file); `pools/set` `{name, standard, accounts}` makes or replaces `[host.pools.NAME]` -> `{"pool", "restart": true}` (`-32003` for an account the host lacks, `-32001` and the reason for a member that does not provide the standard or a name an account or the host's pool has); `pools/remove` `{name}` (refused while an entry offers the pool; `accounts/remove` is refused while a pool holds the account); `pools/turn` `{entry, account, model?, current?, idle_seconds?, lifetime?, limited?: [{account, resets_at?}]}` -> for a pool `{pool, account, moved, why ("start"|"kept"|"in_turn"|"exhausted"), left?, skipped: [{account, why}]}`, refused with the reason (`-32001`) when no account has usage left; for one account `{pool: null, account, moved: false, exhausted, left?, refills_at?, alternatives}`; also `accounts/catalog`, `accounts/list`, `accounts/remove`, `accounts/add`, `accounts/login`, `accounts/entries` (each entry also has `pools`: [{name, standard, automatic, listed, accounts: [{name, listed}]}], the pools it offers), `accounts/offer` (its accounts may name pools): sign in to a provider on this host, which runs the provider's program as the user and keeps a credential in the host's store. A typed answer of the sign-in's form is sealed to the key that asked, so the host opens it itself; a key the trust store does not hold, or a host key secret prompts do not serve, gets a refusal |
| `usage` | `usage/summary` `{from?, to?, group_by?, account?, provider?, model?, session?, host?}` -> `{"from", "to", "group_by", "timezone": "UTC", "total", "groups": [{"key", "records", "sessions", "input"?, "output"?, "cache_read"?, "cache_write"?, "cost_micro_usd"?}]}`: the spend recorded for lines with `from <= time < to` (Unix seconds, default all), grouped by `day` (UTC, `YYYY-MM-DD`, the default), `account`, `provider` (the harness), `model` (`key` null when the agent named none) or `session`. A counter is absent from a group when no line in it has one, never 0. `host` is the client's label for this host: it is read and ignored. `usage/limits` -> `{"accounts": [{"account", "updated_at", "observed_at", "buckets", "limit"?}]}`: the allowances each account last reported, with when the host read them. A host with no home answers `-32002`. The portal host gives the grant to the key it paired for its own person; `canoa serve` only when canoa.cfg names the key; a delegated child connection never has it |
| `terminal` | `terminal/open`, `terminal/write`, `terminal/resize`, `terminal/scrollback`, `terminal/close`, and `terminal/output` and `terminal/exit` pushed back: the person's own shell on this computer, as the user, on a pseudo-terminal (see **A terminal**). It is a shell, so it is as much as everything else this user can do here; `[host.native] control` refuses it, so a browser extension never gets it through the door; a delegated child connection never has it, and what a shell prints reaches no agent. The portal host gives it to the keys that reach a person's own host; `canoa serve` only when canoa.cfg names the key |
| `usage` | also `usage/read` `{"account"}` (an account's name) -> `{"account": row}`: the allowances that account's provider reports now with no session, as a row of `usage/limits` (`account`, `observed_at`, `buckets`, `supported`), kept 60 s. The host reads the plan limits through the account provider's `usage` with the account's credential (Claude and Codex answer; Grok and Pi answer "not offered"; a `harness-login` account is asked the same, Codex reads its program's own allowances and Claude the login in Claude Code's configuration directory (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, else `.claude`), renewed through Claude Code when expired; no session: Claude's login token goes to Claude's OAuth API, a Codex ChatGPT login's to the ChatGPT backend or the account's `chatgpt_base_url`); an expired login (`GONE`) is renewed through the account provider's `renew` (the host writes the stored login file into the account's directory first, since the account provider looks at that directory and never fills it), stored, and read once more, and an account another connection holds is read from the stored copy. An API key or gateway token (a kind ending `-api-key` or `-auth-token`: Claude's, Codex's) has no plan, and reports what it can: buckets read from the rate-limit headers of the provider's model list (`source` `response_headers`: requests, tokens, and for Anthropic input and output tokens), from a LiteLLM-style gateway's `GET /key/info` when the account's `base_url` (Codex: `openai_base_url`) names one (`source` `gateway`: `budget` in `micro_usd` with its reset, `rpm`, `tpm`), and from this host's own usage log (`source` `recorded`: `recorded_today` (UTC), `recorded_30_days` and `recorded_model_<model>`, input plus output tokens, no call to anyone); the key goes only to the account's own `base_url`, where its turns go, and a provider that sends no such headers gives none. A row with `supported: false` carries `unsupported_because` (a Console login, Pi, a local model, or a key whose provider reports nothing and with nothing recorded: not offered); `supported` with no buckets is not reported yet. Any other provider is asked as the account through an entry that runs it (the provider's defaults when none does), and one that learns usage only during a turn answers a row with no buckets. `-32003` for an account canoa.cfg lacks, `-32002` when the host manages no accounts or the account cannot be used |
| `voice` | `speech/providers` `{}` -> `{"types": [{id, title, description, schema, form}], "providers": [{name, provider, settings, secrets_set, models, offer, title?, description?, encodings?, partials?, max_seconds?, hints?, problem?}]}` (`models` is `[{id, label}]` for an endpoint whose address names a service Canoa knows, else empty; `offer` is the offer the table was made from, else null), `speech/offers` `{}` -> `{"offers": [{id, title, summary, where, local, models, default_model, license?, store?, kind?, settings?, installed?, version?, provider, account?, service?, unavailable?}]}` (what Canoa offers to add: `whisper`, a local engine installed through `whisper_store`; `foundry`, a Whisper model of the Foundry Local service on this computer (`service: "foundry_local"`, `models` the Whisper ids the running service lists, `unavailable` saying what is missing when it is not running or lists none); `openai`, a `speech_endpoint` preset with its models, for a key typed in; and one `openai_account:NAME` for each account of a provider one of whose account kinds is a key for the OpenAI API at OpenAI's own address (the kind says it: it provides the `openai-api` standard and its model API's default address is OpenAI's) (`account: NAME`, the title and `where` name it so the person knows which key pays), `unavailable` set for one that is not an OpenAI API key; `provider` is the name of the table made from it, else null; `where` says in words where the audio goes), `speech/open` `{provider, rate?, channels?, encoding?, language?, vocabulary?, profile?, model?}` -> `{"session", "partials"}` (`model` replaces the `model` setting of an endpoint provider for this recording), `speech/push` `{session, audio}` (base64, at most 1 MiB a call) -> `{"partial"}`, `speech/finish` `{session}` -> `{"text", "front_matter"}`, `speech/cancel` `{session}`: audio goes to a speech provider the person set up in `[speech.NAME]` and the text comes back (see **Speech providers**). A key reaches only the recordings it opened (4 at a time, 16 on the host), and one nobody has called for in 120 s is ended. The same grant opens the other direction, text to speech: `speech/voices` `{provider?, language?}` -> `{"types": [{id, title, description, schema, form}], "providers": [{name, provider, builtin, settings, secrets_set, title, description, encodings, models: [{id, label, description?}], default_model?, styles: [...], speed?: {min, max}, uses, leaves, endpoint?, words?, max_chars?, voices: [{id, name, language?, gender?, description?, models}], voices_problem?, problem?}]}`, `speech/say` `{provider, text, voice?, model?, style?, speed?, language?}` -> `{"utterance", "encoding", "rate", "channels"}`, `speech/audio` `{utterance, wait_ms?, max_bytes?}` -> `{"audio", "done", "error"?}` (base64, at most 256 KiB a call; waits up to `wait_ms`, default 500, at most 2000, for audio; `done` once the utterance is over, `error` when it ended in a failure, after the audio it made) and `speech/stop` `{utterance}` (see **Speech output**). A key reaches only the utterances it started (4 at a time, 16 on the host), and one nobody has called for in 120 s is ended |
| `state` | `state/get` `{name, offset?}` -> `{data, size, done}` (base64, at most 512 KiB a call), `state/put` `{name, data, offset?, done?}` (whole, or in chunks with `done: false` until the last and `offset` where the document stands; the old document stays until the new is whole), `state/append` `{name, text}`, `state/list` `{prefix?}` -> `{names}` and `state/remove` `{name}` (a document, or every one under a folder's name): documents under `<home>/state/`, named by up to four parts of letters, digits, `.`, `_` and `-` joined by `/`, none starting with a dot, 512 MiB together. They are shared by every key that holds the grant, not owned by one, so what a page would lose with its browser (drafts, the layout, the permission rules and their log) follows the person to another. A remembered rule decides what an agent may do unasked, so it is as much as access to the agent; `[host.native] control` does not default to it. A host with no home answers `-32002` |
| `voice.setup` | `speech/configure` `{name, provider, settings?, clear?}` or `{name, remove: true}` (and `speech/say_configure`, the same for a `[say.NAME]` table): writes or removes a `[speech.NAME]` table, which names a program this host runs as the user or an address it sends audio to, so it is as much as access to the agent; `speech/enable` `{offer: "whisper", name?}` -> `{name, provider, model, saved}`: writes the table of the offer from the copy `whisper_store` installed (`-32002` when none is, or it holds no model); `[host.native] control` refuses it |
| `tools.provide` | opens `@tools` (the door's, or `Local::open("@tools")`) to offer a program's tools to the host's sessions; what such a provider answers reaches an agent as its tools' own results, so it is as much as access to the agent's context. Not a control method |
| `tools.use` | the sessions of a connection whose key holds it are given the tools providers offer. The app's own link holds it; a paired key only through `[host.control]`; never a task another agent handed out, and never a session a web page started. Not a control method |
| `mail` | the sessions of a connection whose key holds it are given the `canoa-mail` tools (**The mail tools**): the person's mail, searched, read, drafted into, and organized with the person's yes to each change. The app's own link holds it; a paired key only through `[host.control]` or `[host.native] control`; never a portal's keys, a task another agent handed out, or a session a web page started. Not a control method |
| `mail.setup` | `mail/accounts` `{}` -> `{"accounts": [{name, backend, server, email, user, state}], "tools"}` (`state` is `password stored`, `needs a password`, `password from VAR`, `needs VAR set`, `signs in with the Windows logon` or `signed in by Outlook`; never a password), `mail/add` `{name, backend: "ews"|"outlook", email?, server?, autodiscover?, user?, profile?, timeout_secs?, password?}` -> `{"account"}` (what `canoa mail add` does: a user name needs the `password`, which goes to the account store and is answered nowhere; the table is written with an `id`), `mail/test` `{name}` -> `{"ok": true, "folders"}` or `{"ok": false, "problem"}` (the host signs in with what it holds and lists the folders; a sign-in that fails is an answer, not an error; `-32003` for an unknown name), `mail/remove` `{name}` -> `{"removed"}`. Each change makes the mail tools' mailboxes again at once, so a session that starts afterwards has the account. Held by the app's own link; a paired key only through `[host.control]` or `[host.native] control`; never a portal's keys. It is as much as access to the person's mail: the account names the server the host then connects to with the password the key typed |
| `secrets` | `secrets/providers` `{}` -> `{"providers": [{id, title, description, origin, can_store, can_list, needs_person, schema, form}], "entries": [{name, provider, keep, settings, from}]}` (what the host offers and the entries `[secrets]` holds), `secrets/list` `{}` -> `{"secrets": [{provider, name, kept: "session" or "process" or "stored", session, expires_in}], "keeps": [{id, label}]}` (the cache and what a provider that keeps and lists says it keeps: names, where, how long, never a value), `secrets/reveal` `{provider, name}` -> `{"value"}` (the one call that answers a value, for the person who asked to see it; `-32003` when the entry holds none), `secrets/forget` `{provider, name}` -> `{"forgot": bool}` (the cache, and the provider's own store when it keeps), `secrets/configure` `{name, provider, keep?, settings?, from?}` -> `{"name", "saved"}` (sets up the entry `name` of `[secrets.providers]`: `settings` are plain data the provider declares, a secret setting is refused with a reason that does not repeat it and is given as `from: {setting: {entry, name}}` instead, only for a setting the provider marks `x-from-secret`; the whole section must read, name a provider this host offers and make an instance, or nothing is written) or `{"name", "remove": true}` -> `{"removed"}` (refused while a name, the default or the store entry still points at it), and the long `secrets/set` `{provider, name}` -> `{"set": true}`: the host puts a form (the value, and how long to keep it when the entry keeps nothing itself) to the person sealed to the key that asked, on the same link as `_coqui_acp/secret_request`, and sets what comes back in the entry's store or in memory as chosen; a form declined, a link that ends or no answer in the ask wait sets nothing. A secret is whatever an agent may be given a handle to use, so this is as much as access to them: a key never holds it unless canoa.cfg gives it, and the application in this process does. |
| `hosting` | `host/keys` -> `{"keys": [{id, state: "accepted" or "waiting", source?}]}`, `host/accept` `{id}` -> `{"accepted"}` (a waiting key only; `-32002` for one that is not waiting), `host/revoke` `{id}` -> `{"removed"}`, and `host/pair` `{native?, listen?, origin?, timeout_seconds?}` (default 300 s; `listen` defaults to `127.0.0.1:0`, `native` is the browser extension's door in the home, and the two exclude each other): shows a PIN as one `host/progress` notification (`id`, `pin`, `peer`, `method`, and `url` or `native`) on the link, then answers `{"paired": id}` with the key that used it. One wrong try, the timeout or the link closing ends it, and a link serves other requests meanwhile but not a second `host/pair`; four run at once on a host. `config/get`, `config/set` and `config/check` with `section: "host"` read, replace (null removes) and check `[host]`, which is not one of the preference sections; `config/set` and `config/check` answer in the host's words (`-32002`) when canoa.cfg would then be refused, and `config/check` writes nothing (`{"valid": true}`). The host reads `[host]` only when it starts (`restart: true`). `config/get` of `host` also answers a `digest` naming the table as read; a `config/set` that sends it back as `digest` is refused with `-32004` when the file holds another `[host]` by then (nothing is written), and answers the table's new `digest` when it writes. A paired key reaches what its own grants let it, and `[host]` names the programs this host runs and the grants its keys hold, so it is as much as access to the agent; `[host.native] control` refuses it |
| *(none)* | `options/choices` `{"entry", "account"?}` -> what `canoa options ENTRY --entry --format json` prints: the models and efforts the account provider of the entry's account lists before a session starts (an `account_provider`'s `models`, asked of the provider of the account's kind, the `local` account when none is named; a Foundry Local account lists its engine's models; a program that lists only in its own session is probed once per version; `Claude` and `Codex` with no entry in canoa.cfg are the built-in sources). `account` is one the entry offers (or a pool, whose models are those its accounts list, each once, with `accounts` naming those that list it, and a top-level `accounts` saying which listed and why one did not), and a key holding `accounts` (the owner included) may name any other account of the provider; the answer is asked as that account, so it is not `signed_out` for a signed-in one. No grant: the owner, and a key the entry's `peers` let start a session (a built-in source with no entry only on a portal host), may ask; `-32001` otherwise, `-32003` for a name the host has no source for. A delegated child connection has no control channel |
| *(none)* | `host/grants` `{}` -> `{"grants": [names]}`: what the asking key holds (the owner, all of them), so an application shows only what it can use |
| *(none)* | `history/export`, `history/move`, `history/portable`, `history/importable`, `history/import`, `history/imported`: **owner only**; no grant names them |

**Speech providers** (`[speech.NAME]`, grants `voice` and `voice.setup`) turn audio a client captured into text on this host, so a page and every platform
reach one. A table names a kind, `provider = "speech_command"` (a program that reads the audio on its standard input and writes text: a script of the Canoa SDK,
`CommandSpeech`, that ships in the host and runs in its JavaScript source) or `"speech_endpoint"` (an HTTP speech-to-text address and model, `canoa::speech::builtin`'s
`EndpointSettings`), and that kind's settings, which are read when a method is called, so a change in the file applies at once. A table may instead name where its
provider's code comes from, as an entry of canoa.cfg does: `library` (a Guatiao library file) or `source` (a Guatiao source file, or `bundled:js` for the host's own QuickJS,
with `source_settings` such as `dirs`, and `import_search`; relative paths are taken from canoa.cfg's folder), and `provider` is then an id that registry offers: the
`[say.NAME]` tables of text to speech take the same keys. Those keys are the host's, not settings of the provider: `speech/providers` lists the table's `settings`
without them and `speech/configure` keeps them when the settings are saved. A library or source runs as this host does, so naming one is the person's, in canoa.cfg; a
table naming both, or an origin without the provider, is listed with `problem`. `speech/providers` answers each kind's JSON Schema and its guatiao-intake form, so a
settings screen draws them as it draws any provider's, and every table with what the provider says of itself or why it cannot be built; a setting the schema marks
a secret (an endpoint's key) is never answered, only listed in `secrets_set`. The table is not a section `config/set` or `config/get` reaches, because it names
programs and holds keys: `speech/configure` writes it (a setting the schema does not name is refused; a secret left out or empty keeps what the table had,
`clear` names the ones to take out; what it saves must build). A recording is `speech/open`, `speech/push` as often as chunks arrive (the call returns when the
provider has taken the chunk, so a slow provider slows the sender), then `speech/finish` (the text and its front matter) or `speech/cancel`. Programs start
through the host's own spawner.

A table may carry `offer = "whisper"`, which says Canoa made it from an offer: it is not one of the kind's settings, `speech/providers` answers it as `offer`
(and a client that knows the offer is local prefers it when the person never chose a service), and `speech/configure` keeps it when the settings are saved.
`speech/enable` `{offer, name?, model?}` makes the table of an offer that needs no form. `foundry` (`model` required, a Foundry model name) writes `provider = "speech_endpoint"`, `model`, `service = "foundry_local"`, `offer = "foundry"`: no address, which changes with each start of the service, so the host reads the running service's from `~/.foundry/daemon.json` (as `canoa-foundry` does) whenever a recording opens, and calls `/v1/audio/transcriptions` there with `request = "local_file"`. `service_address = "http://127.0.0.1:PORT"` in a table names the service instead. The model is never loaded by Canoa: `speech/providers` answers `problem` while the service is not running, and the service's own words come back when the model is not loaded. `openai_account:NAME` writes an endpoint table for OpenAI's `https://api.openai.com/v1/audio/transcriptions` with `account = "NAME"` and the model (`gpt-transcribe` by default); the key is read from the account's store (a kind that provides `openai-api`, credential `api_key`) when a recording opens and is never written to canoa.cfg or answered. Only accounts of such a kind whose setting that moves the model API's address (the kind's endpoint slot names it) is unset (or `https://api.openai.com/v1`) are usable; a ChatGPT sign-in (`codex-chatgpt`, `codex-access-token`) is not an API key and is listed with that reason, since this was not measured against the live service. The default name is `openai-NAME`; a table that names a removed account is listed with `problem`. `account`, `service`, `service_address` and `offer` are the host's keys of a table, not settings of its kind, and `speech/configure` keeps them when the settings are saved.
`speech/enable` for `whisper` writes the whisper table from the installed copy: `speech_command` running `whisper-cli -m <model> -f - -of canoa -nt -np -l
{language_code|auto} --prompt {vocabulary}` with a WAV stream on its standard input (`-of` only makes the program print to its standard output, which it does
not for a stream otherwise; nothing is written to a file). A model is chosen by installing it; a service's model is `speech/open`'s `model`, applied to
`speech_endpoint` providers only.

**Speech output** (`[say.NAME]`, grants `voice` and `voice.setup`) is the other direction: the host turns text into audio for a client that has no voices of its own, so a page and every platform can read a reply aloud. A table names a kind, `provider = "speech_say_command"` (a program that reads text and writes audio), `"speech_say_endpoint"` (an HTTP address in the shape of OpenAI's `audio/speech`), `"speech_say_piper"` (Piper over a folder of voices) or `"speech_say_system"` (the operating system's own voices: Windows PowerShell's desktop synthesizer, `say` on macOS, `espeak-ng` elsewhere), and that kind's settings (`canoa::speech::builtin`'s `CommandSaySettings`, `EndpointSaySettings`, `PiperSettings` and `SystemSettings`), read when a method is called. Two providers need no table: `system` is always offered, and `piper` when the Store installed Piper (a `[say.piper]` table whose `program` or `voices_dir` is empty takes the Store's copy and its `voices` folder); a table of the same name replaces either. `speech/voices` answers each kind's JSON Schema and form, so a settings screen draws them as it draws any provider's, and every provider with what it says of itself (`models`, `styles`, `speed`, which request fields it `uses`), where the text goes (`leaves` `no`, `maybe` or `yes`, the `endpoint` when there is one and `words` saying it in a sentence) and its voices, which are asked of the provider at most every 30 s (`voices_problem` says why they could not be); a setting the schema marks a secret (an endpoint's key) is never answered, only listed in `secrets_set`. `speech/say_configure` writes the table under `voice.setup` as `speech/configure` does. An utterance is `speech/say` (the provider starts; a voice, model or style it does not list is refused `-32002` naming the ones it has), then `speech/audio` for each next piece: the client pulls, the provider holds back at its own buffer, so a player that is behind slows the provider and nothing grows here. The audio is 16-bit little-endian PCM at the `rate` and `channels` `speech/say` answered. `done` ends it; `speech/stop` ends it early; an utterance nobody called for in 120 s is ended. Programs start through the host's own spawner.

**A terminal** (`terminal/*`, grant `terminal`) is a shell the host starts for the person on a pseudo-terminal (ConPTY on Windows 10 1809 and later, `openpt` on Unix), with nothing filtered, restricted or confirmed beyond the grant. It
belongs to the link that opened it and needs one that can carry notifications (a request sent to `handle` alone is refused `-32002`); when the link ends, or on
`terminal/close`, the shell and everything it started end (a job object on Windows, the shell's process group on Unix: a process that left its group on its own is not reached).

- `terminal/open` `{"cols"?, "rows"?, "cwd"?, "shell"?}` (`shell` an id `shell/list` gave, never a program; one this host did not find is refused `-32002` naming `shell/list`; cells 1 to 1000, default 80 by 24; `cwd` an absolute folder that exists, default the user's home) -> `{"id", "shell", "cwd", "notice"?}`; `cwd` may be a folder on a share, answered as `\\server\share\...`, and `notice` says when the shell (`cmd.exe`) cannot start there and opened in its own folder. The shell is the request's `shell`, else `[host] shell` (an id of `shell/list`, else a program), else `$SHELL`, else `/bin/sh` (Windows: `pwsh.exe`, `powershell.exe`, `%COMSPEC%`); a request names an id of the list and never a program. On Windows a bare name is found on `PATH` (absolute folders only) and the shell is started by its absolute path, never from the folder the host is in; a path may hold spaces. At most 8 terminals open on a link (ended ones count until closed, 32 at most) and 32 on a host, refused `-32002` with the limit.
- `terminal/write` `{"id", "data"}`: `data` is base64 of at most 16 KiB of what was typed. It is queued (32 writes) for a thread that writes to the shell, so a shell that is not reading holds up only its own terminal: a full queue is refused `-32002`, and the person sends again.
- `terminal/resize` `{"id", "cols", "rows"}`. `terminal/scrollback` `{"id"}` -> `{"data", "end"}`: the latest 256 KiB the shell printed, base64, and `end`, the number of bytes printed since it started. `terminal/close` `{"id"}` -> `{"closed": true}`.
- Pushed, with no `id` of a request: `{"method":"terminal/output","params":{"id","data","end"}}` as the shell writes (at most 16 KiB of bytes in a message, base64; `end` is the position just after them, so a view that read the scrollback drops what it already has), and `{"method":"terminal/exit","params":{"id","code"}}` once the shell has ended (a signal is 128 plus its number; not sent after `terminal/close`). Nothing is read ahead of the link: the host writes a chunk and waits for the link to take it, so a slow client slows the shell and the host holds no more than the scrollback; a link whose queue is full is waited for, and the shell is ended only when the link has ended.
- Errors: `-32602` a field is not well formed, `-32003` this link has no such terminal, `-32002` a limit, a shell that did not start (the reason names the program) or an ended terminal.

**A page's files** (`fs/list`, `config/*`, `upload/*`, `host/restart`) are what a web page, which cannot touch the disk or run a
program, asks its host to do as the user. Each is behind its own grant, so a key given only the ledger gets none of them.

- `fs/list` `{"path"?: absolute, "hidden"?: bool, "files"?: bool}` -> `{"path", "parent", "entries": [{"name", "path", "dir"}], "more"}`,
  folders only unless `files`, dot names only with `hidden`, folders first then by name, at most `MAX_LIST` (1000, `more` says so).
  With no `path` it lists the places to start from: for a key the user's home and the working roots of the entries the key may use
  (all keys when an entry has no `peers`); for the owner every drive or `/`. A path must be absolute with no `.` or `..` and, once
  every link is resolved, under one of those places; a path outside them and one that is not there are the same answer (`-32003`).
  An entry whose link leads out of the places is not listed. `parent` is null where the parent is outside them. Every name is
  listed, with each control, direction or invisible character shown as `<U+XXXX>` (`path` is the real one). A key that also holds
  `files` is not kept to those places: it lists whatever the host's account can, anywhere (the places to start from are then the
  home and every drive or `/`), any absolute path, links and network shares included, and `parent` is offered wherever it is.
- `fs/read` `{"path": absolute, "max_bytes"?: positive whole number}` -> `{"path", "name", "size", "kind": "text"|"image"|"binary", "mime": string|null, "truncated", "data": base64|null}`, the start of one regular file, for a preview (grant `files`). It reads what the host's account can read, wherever it is: any drive, a network share or a link the operating system follows. `max_bytes` defaults to `DEFAULT_READ_BYTES` (256 KiB) and is cut to `MAX_READ_BYTES` (512 KiB), the most a page's transport carries once in base64; `size` is the whole file and `truncated` says the bytes stop short. `kind` is `text` (valid UTF-8 with no zero byte; a character cut off by the limit does not make it binary), `image` (PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP by their first bytes, never SVG; a cut image carries no `data`, and the answer says nothing of its dimensions, so the client bounds what it decodes) or `binary` (no `data`). `name` shows every control, direction and invisible character as `<U+XXXX>`. The only refusals are for what cannot be read as a file, each with its reason in the message: `-32602` for a path that is not absolute, a folder, anything that is not a regular file (a pipe, a socket or a device, which could wait for ever; the file is also opened without waiting, in case the name became one after it was looked at) and `max_bytes` that is not a positive whole number; `-32003` when there is no such file or the account cannot see it; `-32002` with the operating system's own words when it cannot open or read it.

- `fs/info` `{"path"?: absolute, "paths"?: [absolute]}` (at most 64 in all) -> `{"folders": [{"path", "exists", "folder", "repository", "root": string|null, "why"?}]}` (grant `folders`): each place as asked, whether it is there and a folder, and whether it is in a git repository (it or a folder above it holds `.git`, a folder or a file) with that folder as `root` (null for a key when the root is outside the places it may browse). A place the key may not see is `exists: false` with `why`.
- `fs/mentions` `{"path": absolute, "max_entries"?: 5000 (at most 20000), "max_depth"?: 8 (at most 32)}` -> `{"path", "entries": ["a.txt", "src/", "src/main.rs"]}` (grant `folders`, the places `fs/list` keeps to): what an `@` offers inside a session's folder, relative with `/`, a folder ending in `/`, nearest first. In a git repository the files git lists (`ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard`, 10 seconds, 16 MiB, run with every `GIT_*` variable removed and `core.fsmonitor` off, so nothing from the repository runs), else the folder is walked to `max_depth`. Links are neither followed nor listed; a name with a control, direction or invisible character is left out; a path that is not a folder is `-32003`.
- `fs/resolve` `{"path": absolute folder, "name": relative}` -> `{"path", "folder", "size"}` (grant `folders`): the file or folder `name` names inside the folder. A name that climbs (`..`), is absolute, has a drive, a backslash or a control character, is not there, or is or leads through a link out of the folder is `-32003`.
- `fs/realpath` `{"path"?: absolute, "paths"?: [absolute]}` (at most 64 in all) -> `{"paths": [{"path", "real": string|null, "why"?}]}` (grant `folders`): each path with the links followed in the deepest part of it that exists and what is not there yet after it as written, the way the app judges where a remembered rule for a path reaches. `real` is null, with `why`, for a path that is not absolute or has `.`, `..` or a control character, a link that cannot be followed (a dangling one), nothing of it found, and for a key not holding `files`, a place outside the home and the working roots of its entries, or one a link inside them leads out of.
- `fs/repo_root` `{"path"?, "paths"?: [absolute folders]}` (at most 64) -> `{"folders": [{"path", "root": string|null}]}` (grant `folders`): the nearest folder at or above each (which need not exist) that holds `.git`. A linked worktree or a submodule, whose `.git` is a file naming `<main>/.git/worktrees/<name>`, answers `<main>`, so worktrees of one repository have one root. Null for none, and for a key not holding `files` when the root is outside its places.
- `fs/guards` `{}` -> `{"home": string|null, "config": string|null}` (grant `folders`): this user's home folder and the folder holding canoa.cfg, which no remembered rule for a path may reach.
- `fs/scratch` `{"session", "source"?, "handler"?, "now"?}` -> `{"path", "made"}` (grant `folders`) makes the folder the person's own `[sessions] workspace` (canoa.cfg; `${documents}/Canoa/scratch/${session}` when it has none) names for a chat, private to the user on Unix; `fs/scratch_remove` takes the same and removes that folder only when it is empty (`{"path", "removed"}`); `fs/scratch_preview` `{"template", "session"?, ...}` -> `{"path"}` only works out what a template being edited would make and makes nothing. `session` is letters, digits and hyphens, at most 36; `source` and `handler` are each made one safe path segment; `now` is the person's local time as `2026-11-17T10:30:05` (this clock's UTC when absent). A template needs `${session}` and only the variables `documents home session date time datetime handler source host fqdn`.
- `profile/canonical` `{"path"}` -> `{"directory": string|null}` (grant `overlay.read`): the folder with its links resolved, when it is an overlay's (under the overlays folder, or one `[overlays]` names with `dir`; a relative path is read from the folder holding canoa.cfg) or a place `fs/list` shows this key. `profile/tree` `{"directory"}` -> `{"directory", "files": {path: base64}}` (grant `overlay.read`): an overlay's folder read whole as its tree digest counts it (no link followed, `.git` and the like left out, at most 4096 files and 64 MiB, and 24 MiB in one answer); any other folder is refused. `profile/project` `{"directory"}` -> `{"directory", "profile": base64|null}` (grant `folders`, the places `fs/list` keeps to): the folder's resolved name and its `.canoa/profile.toml`, which is null when it has none (at most 1 MiB).
- `providers/list` -> `{"providers": [row], "usage_readers": [{id, origin}], "gateways": [{kind, vendor, provider, title}]}` (grant `config.read`): the agents the host has loaded, as the providers it loaded say of them. A row is made of the `acp_harness`, `account_provider` and `agent_vendor` providers the bundled agents' libraries (`bundled.rs`) and the libraries and sources of canoa.cfg's agent entries and extensions offer, so an agent that is not loaded has no row. A row: `{id, title, program, vendor, aos, model_variable, standards: [{id, title}], names, markers, modes: [{id, title}], mark (SVG text or null), look (the id of its look document or null), harness (whether an `acp_harness` provider runs it), imports (whether the host can bring in the sessions its own program kept), accounts: {provider, title, description, kinds: [{id, title}], logins: [{id, title, produces}], usage: [kind ids; harness-login when the provider reads its program's own], local (whether it offers the harness's own login as the automatic `local` account)} or null, source (the name of the source the host serves of its own for it, or null), origin (`bundled`, or the entry or extension that loads it)}`. The bundled agents come first, in the order their crates are listed, then the others as they were found. `usage_readers` are the `usage_reader` providers the same libraries and sources offer (the ids an agent entry's `usage` may name), each with its `origin` as a row's, the bundled ones first and an id once, as the first registry that offers it; the app's Add an agent lists them.
- `profile_items/list` -> `{"items": [item]}` (grant `config.read`): the things a profile may set, as the providers the host loaded say of them: the `profile_item` providers the host carries (`origin` `built-in`: the model, effort, mode, starting instructions and access; `bundled`: a proxy and an identity, JavaScript libraries written with the Canoa SDK (`extensions/profile_items/`, run by the QuickJS the host carries): the proxy item only says which network its values ask for, the proxy is the host's; the files of the items and the sources are kept under the user's own home, `<home>/bundled/NAME-VERSION-DIGEST/`, by `scripts::Bundle` and `scripts::root()`, a temporary folder in this crate's own tests) and those the libraries and sources of canoa.cfg's agent entries and extensions offer (`origin` is the entry's or extension's name). An item: `{id, title, help, origin, stored, harnesses, schema, form}`, the schema a JSON Schema of the values it takes (`null` for none) and the form the guatiao-intake document that shows it (`null` for none); `stored` is `setting` for one of the session's own settings, which a profile keeps under its own key (`model = ...`, `access = {...}`), and `item` for the others, kept at `[profiles.NAME.items.ID]`. An item whose id an earlier provider took, or whose description is unusable, is not listed. An item a profile holds values for that nothing offers is kept and not applied.
- `settings/sections` -> `{"sections": [section]}` (no grant: the application's own pages are the person's own preferences, so any key reads them; a key without `config.read` is listed only the `built-in` ones, and the pages a provider adds, whose forms hold what the host keeps, need `config.read`): the pages and groups of Settings, as `settings_section` providers say of them: first the sections the application draws itself (`origin` `built-in`: General, Appearance, Notifications, Voice, Shortcuts, Sources, Profiles, MCP servers, Permissions, Accounts, Mail, Shells, Usage, Store, Extensions and About, in that order, each with a heading, icon and search words and no schema: the app picks the widget by the id, and leaves out one whose controller its build or key lacks), then the bundled libraries' and those of canoa.cfg's entries and extensions (`origin` is `bundled` or the entry's or extension's name). The app shows the `built-in` pages from `app/lib/settings/built_in_sections.dart` (what the host lists of its own, written and checked by `CANOA_WRITE_SECTIONS=1 cargo test -p canoa-host --lib settings_sections`) until the host answers and for good when it cannot or refuses, so Settings is never empty. A section: `{id, title, help, icon, group, under, keywords, origin, stored, schema, form}`; `stored` is `config` (the host keeps the values in `[settings.ID]` of canoa.cfg) or `provider`; `schema` and `form` are the guatiao schema and guatiao-intake form the app draws. An id an earlier provider took, or a description that cannot be used, is not listed; a section is gone from the list with its provider, and the values stay in canoa.cfg. `settings/section_get` `{id}` -> `{id, values, secrets_set}` (grant `config.read`): what the section holds, a member the schema marks sensitive left out and named in `secrets_set`, and `secrets_problem` (words with no value) when a secret could not be asked of its provider. For a section kept in `config` the host keeps each sensitive member as the named secret `settings/ID/MEMBER` in the provider `[secrets] accounts` names (the operating system's store by default, as for the accounts' credentials), never in canoa.cfg: a secret an earlier host left in `[settings.ID]` is moved there the first time the section is read or saved (and one that cannot be moved stays and refuses the save, rather than being dropped); a `provider` section is handed its secrets, which it keeps itself. `settings/section_set` `{id, values, clear?}` -> `{id, saved: true}` (grant `config.write`): checks `values` (an object, at most 64 KiB, members the schema names, each of its type, the required ones there) and keeps them, replacing the section's values; a sensitive member left out or empty keeps what was kept unless `clear` names it (a secret kept as above is checked against the schema with its kept value, and a provider that cannot answer leaves it out of the check). The grant `config.write` is as much as the app's own preferences: a `provider` section's `write` runs the provider's code, which the host's own extensions already do.
- `panel_tabs/list` -> `{"tabs": [tab]}` (grant `config.read`): the kinds of tab the application's panel can open, as the `panel_tab` providers the host carries (`origin` `built-in`: `terminal`, a shell on the host, and `logs`, the host's own log) and those the libraries and sources of canoa.cfg's entries and extensions offer (`origin` is the entry's or extension's name) say of them. A tab: `{id, title, help, icon, shows, shell, url, origin}`, `shows` `terminal`, `log` or `page` (`url` is the page's loopback address; the app offers a page only when its host runs on the same computer, and opens it in the person's browser). An id an earlier provider took, or a description that cannot be used, is not listed. `panel_tabs/tail` `{id, lines?}` -> `{id, text}` (grant `hosting`): the latest `lines` (500 by default, at most 5000) lines of the log kind `id`, oldest first; a kind that shows no log, or none by that name, is refused. The built-in log is `canoa_host::host_log`, the last 2000 lines the `canoa` program or the application's log routing wrote, without colour codes.
- `looks/list` -> `{"looks": [document]}` (grant `config.read`): the colour-token documents the loaded agents carry (an `agent_vendor` provider's `look`), the bundled agents' in their order. A document is `{id, label, vendor, light, dark}`; `id` is the family the app names (`claude`, `chatgpt`), `vendor` the provider whose conversations it draws (`claude`, `codex`), and each brightness holds `#RRGGBB` strings for `page`, `rail`, `bar`, `selected`, `surface`, `raised`, `text`, `muted`, `accent`, `onAccent`, `success`, `warning`, `danger` (optional `border`), the numbers `radius`, `controlRadius`, `railWidth`, and a `chat` table of `user`, `notice`, `added`, `removed`, `code`. Nothing in them is secret. A client ignores a token it does not know and takes its own default for one that is missing.
- `trust/list` -> `{"projects": [{"directory", "sha256", "trusted"?}]}` (grant `config.read`), `trust/set` `{"directory", "sha256"}` (grant `config.write.sessions`) and `trust/revoke` `{"directory"}` (grant `config.write`) -> the same list: the projects whose profiles the person trusts, kept in `trust.json` beside canoa.cfg. `trust/set` reads the profile now and refuses one whose SHA-256 is not the one named, so what is trusted is what was shown; a folder with no profile cannot be trusted; revoking needs no folder to be there.
- `config/get` `{"section"}` -> `{"section", "value": object|null, "restart": false}` and `config/set` `{"section", "value": object|null}` (the app this host runs in, the owner link, alone also reaches `agents`, `desktop`, `profile`, `skill_pins`; no paired key does)
  -> `{"section", "restart"}` read and write one table of the person's own canoa.cfg (the file `--config` lists last), in the format
  it is in (a `.yaml`/`.json` name or a `#!` first line, else TOML; comments and layout are not kept). Only `SECTIONS` are named
  (`appearance assets attachments catalogs connections defaults loadouts network notifications overlays permissions sessions store usage`); any other, `host`,
  `agents`, `profile`, `desktop` and `skill_pins` among them, is `-32001`, since those name programs to run or what may run. `value` is
  an object of at most `MAX_SECTION_BYTES` (256 KiB) or null, which removes the table. The text that would result is read by the host's
  own loader first, and a file it would refuse is not written (`-32002`); the text is read back and no other table may differ; the write is beside the file first, then put in its place keeping the mode (Unix) or access list (Windows) the file had, and through the link when canoa.cfg is one. `restart` is
  true for the sections the host itself reads at start (`permissions usage`): they apply after `host/restart`; `[overlays]`, `[profile]`, `[loadouts]` and `[defaults]` reach the next session of an entry that is the host's own or sets `own_profile`, with no restart. A host not
  started with a canoa.cfg refuses both (`-32002`).
- `upload/put` `{"name", "data": base64, "done"?: bool}` -> `{"id", "path", "name", "size", "done"}` keeps a file in
  `control/uploads/<digest of the key's id>/` (`0700`; the file `0600`) as `<12 hex digits>-<name>`, the name cut to its last
  component and to 100 bytes with anything but letters, digits and `.-_ ` made `_`. A chunk is at most `MAX_CHUNK` (512 KiB), since a
  page's transport carries no message over a mebibyte: a larger file starts with `done: false`, which answers an `id`, and goes on
  with `{"id", "offset": the size so far, "data", "done"?}` until `done` is true or absent; an `offset` that is not where the upload
  stands, or an `id` that is not this key's, is `-32002`. Until it is done the file is in the folder `.partial`, so a file called `x.part` is only a name. One upload step runs at a time, so the limits are checked and spent together. `max_upload_bytes` (one file;
  past it the upload ends and what was kept goes), `max_upload_total_bytes` and `max_uploads` hold per key (the owner has its own
  folder), past one it is `-32002`; a bad base64 string is `-32602`. An unfinished upload nobody continues for an hour is removed
  when the key next starts one. `upload/remove` `{"id"}` (the `name` upload/put answered; never a path) -> `{"removed"}` removes a file in the caller's own folder,
  and what is left of an unfinished one, and nothing else.
- `host/restart` `{}` -> `{"restarting": true}` is offered only when `serve` was started with `--supervised` (`ServeOptions::with_supervised`), which says a supervisor starts it again; otherwise it is `-32002`, "restart is not available". It ends the host 300 ms later with status `cli::STATUS_RESTART` (75) and stops the web
  server, for a supervisor to start it again: canoa.cfg is read at start. Without a supervisor it is an end.

**Overlays for a page** (`overlay/*`, `catalog/*`) are the git half of the overlay store, for a page that cannot run git. The store's
own logic (reading a catalog, listing it, showing what an overlay would do) stays in the app over these primitives. Installed
overlays are kept where the host already reads them: `overlays/<name>/` (the files), `overlays/<name>.json` (the record: `source`,
`commit`, `digest`, `files` by blob id, `skipped`, `installed`, `catalog`?) beside the person's own canoa.cfg, and the fetched history
in `overlays/.cache/<name>.git` (a catalog's in `overlays/.catalogs/<label>.git`). A name is a valid AOS overlay name of 1 to 64 letters, digits, `-` or `_`.

- `overlay/check` `{"name", "repo", "ref", "path"?, "spec"?, "pin"?: "sha256:…", "catalog"?: LABEL}` (`repo` as canoa.cfg writes it, with or without `git+`; `spec` is the text a record keeps as its source, `repo@ref#path` when none) fetches the source (one commit deep; a whole
  commit id is that object and nothing else; a branch or tag by `ls-remote`) and lists the tree at that commit as git does, never checking
  anything out: a link, a submodule, a name that is not a relative path or two names equal without ASCII case refuse the overlay; entries
  under an excluded name (`.git`, `node_modules`, `__pycache__`, `*.pyc`, …) are left out and reported as `skipped`; more than
  `catalog` says the overlay was chosen from that catalog's listing, not typed by the person: it is then fetched with none of the person's git
  settings, credentials, ssh keys or agent, as `skill/fetch` is (a label is a name as below; `catalog/fetch`, which reads the catalog
  itself, and its `remote_revision` refresh stay the person's own, since canoa.cfg or the default row names that source).
  4096 files, 64 MiB, or 16384 entries is refused. The tree digest is AOS's and must equal `pin` when one is given (`-32002`, nothing
  installed). It answers `{"token", "name", "from", "fromSource", "fromDigest", "commit", "digest", "files": {path: blob id},
  "skipped", "added", "changed", "removed", "manifest"}` and changes nothing installed; the host keeps the plan under `token` for
  its caller, good for 15 minutes, 16 at most.
- `overlay/read` `{"token"}` -> `{"files": {path: base64}}` gives the files a check holds, to show what the overlay would do; more than 8 MiB is `-32002`.
- `overlay/apply` `{"token", "catalog"?}` installs exactly that plan, never a client's list: the files are read from git's object store by id, written
  exclusively into `overlays/.staging-<name>`, digested again as written, and only then does the folder replace the old one whole and
  the record get written beside and renamed. A plan is spent by its first apply and is its key's alone. `catalog` is the label
  the store installed it from, kept for a later apply of the same source.
- `overlay/installed` `{"name"?}` -> `{"overlays": {name: record}}`; `overlay/remove` `{"name"}` deletes folder, record and history;
  `overlay/discard` `{"name"}` deletes what a check fetched for an overlay that is not installed.
- `registry/fetch` `{"url"}` -> `{"body"}`: one page of an MCP registry's list, read by the host so a page need not reach the registry. The address must be `<base>/v0.1/servers` with at most `limit`, `cursor` and `search`, over `https` (or `http` to this computer), with no user information or fragment; no redirect is followed, the registry has 20 seconds, and more than 4 MiB is refused. The person running the host reads any such registry; a key only the official one and the ones `[store.mcps.catalogs]` lists (git registries are read through `catalog/*`). Needs `overlay.write`.
- `network/fetch` `{"url", "method"?, "headers"?: [[name, value]], "body"?: base64, "seconds"?}` -> `{"status", "headers": [[name, value]], "body": base64}`: one HTTP request the app makes, sent from this host so it follows the host's network (the system's proxy with its login, the operating system's certificate verifier, the client certificate of its keystores, `[host.network]`), which the app's own library does not. `http` or `https` only; any status is an answer and a redirect is followed as the network's agent follows one; 1 to 300 seconds (30 unless it says), and at most 16 MiB each way. Only the person running the host (this process's own app) asks for it: a key is refused whatever it holds, since the request leaves as this computer with its logins.
- `network/client_certificates` `{}` -> `{"certificates": [{"thumbprint", "subject", "issuer", "not_after", "source"}]}`: the client certificates this host can present, for a profile's proxy item to offer as a pick for each pattern of hosts (the form's `identity` member carries the widget hint `client_certificate`): `thumbprint` is the SHA-256 of the certificate in lower-case hex, which the item names it by, `not_after` seconds since the Unix epoch, `source` where it is held; the Windows store, the macOS Keychain, a PKCS#11 module where one is set up, or `[host.network] client_certificate`'s file (none for `none`), those expiring last first. Never a key. The owner or a key with `config_write` (the grant that edits profiles).
- `network/tunnel` `{"url", "seconds"?}` -> `{"direct": true}` or `{"port", "token", "tls"?: {name, subject, sha256}}`: the connection the app is about to make to a remote Canoa host (its WebSocket), made by this host so it follows its network: through the proxy it chooses for the address and, for `https`, with the TLS to the server made here. `url` is the `http` or `https` address the app will connect to (only its host and port matter). `http`: `direct` when no proxy applies, so the app connects itself; otherwise this host has already connected through the proxy and logged in (Basic from the settings, Negotiate or NTLM with the logon session, `[host.network]` over it). `https`: never `direct`; this host has connected (through the proxy, or straight to the server) and made the TLS with its network's trust (the operating system's verifier, or exactly `ca_file`) and the client certificate a server asks for from its keystores (or `client_certificate`), and verified the server for the URL's host, so what the app carries is the plain bytes inside the TLS; `tls` says who the server proved to be (`name` is the host verified, `subject` its certificate's subject, `sha256` that certificate's digest) and the app does not go on without its own host's name there. Either way this host then listens on the loopback `port` for the one connection the app makes within `seconds` (1 to 300, 30 unless it says; after that the upstream connection is closed) and carries it both ways until either end closes. That connection must begin with the 32 characters of `token` (one per call, unguessable), which are not passed on; a connection that does not is dropped, so another process on this computer that finds the port is not carried, and with it the person's client certificate and what the server sends are not its to use. A proxy that refuses the login, a server that cannot be reached, a certificate that is not trusted or does not match the host, or a SOCKS proxy is `-32002` with the reason. Only the person running the host (this process's own app) asks for it: a key is refused whatever it holds. The peer proof the remote host requires is still end to end inside the WebSocket; the TLS ends at this host.
- `skill/search` `{"query", "limit"?}` -> `{"from", "skills": [{id, source, skillId, name, installs}]}`: the skills a directory lists for a query (2 to 128 characters; `limit` 1 to 50, 20 by default), most installed first, asked as `<base>/api/search?q=<query>&limit=<n>`. It answers only while `[store] skills_search` in the person's canoa.cfg is `true` (skills.sh) or the address of another directory (`https`, or `http` to this computer, with no user information, query or fragment); absent or `false` is off, for a key as for the person, and nothing is asked. The directory is a third party whose search is undocumented, so no redirect is followed, it has 20 seconds, more than 1 MiB is refused, and the answer is read as data: a result is kept only with a `source` that is a GitHub `owner/repo` and a `skillId` that is a plain name, and only those members. A result is where to look, never files: installing one goes through `skill/scan` and `skill/fetch` on `source`. Needs `overlay.write`.
- `skill/*`: skills fetched from elsewhere, kept under `overlays/.skills/` as `<lower-case name>/` (the files the skill's digest covers) and `<lower-case name>.json` (what the app accepted), the layout the profile reader checks again before it delivers one.
  `skill/list` -> `{"skills": {key: {name, source, ref?, pin?, digest, catalog?, overlays, chosen}}}` (no files; a record that names another skill than its file is left out) and `skill/read` `{"name"}` -> `{"files": {path: base64}}` (none when the folder holds a link or passes 1024 files or 16 MiB) need `overlay.read`.
  `skill/fetch` `{"repo", "ref"?, "path"?}` -> `{"commit", "files": {path: base64}}` fetches the source one commit deep with git that has none of the person's settings (no global or system configuration, URL rewrites or credential helper, no ssh configuration, key or agent, no redirect followed, every object checked, `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, transports `https`, `http`, `git`, `ssh`, `file`), since the source was named by an overlay and not the person (no `ref` is the repository's head), leaves out what is under an excluded name, refuses a link or more than a skill may hold, and keeps nothing; with `"own": true` (the app says the person typed the source themselves) it is fetched as the person would from a shell, with their git settings, credentials and keys, as `overlay/check` fetches a source with no `catalog`; `skill/scan` `{"repo", "ref"?, "path"?, "own"?}` -> `{"commit", "skills": [{"name", "description", "path", "problem"?}], "skipped", "truncated"}` fetches the source the same way (`own` as for `skill/fetch`) and lists the skills it holds as `npx skills` finds them, from each `SKILL.md`'s front matter alone and no other file of any skill: the source's own `SKILL.md` when it has one, else those directly inside `skills/`, `skills/.curated`, `.experimental`, `.system` and the folders agents read (`.claude/skills`, `.agents/skills`, ...), else any within five folders; `path` is where in the source to start, and each skill's `path` is from its root, as `skill/fetch` takes it. A skill whose name is not a skill name or not its folder's carries a `problem`; a `SKILL.md` that is no skill (front matter that does not name and describe it), one with `metadata.internal: true` and a repeated name are left out and counted in `skipped`; at most 1024 are listed (`truncated`). Nothing is kept; `skill/save` `{name, source, ref?, pin?, digest, catalog?, overlays, chosen, files: {path: base64}}` writes the record and files only when they pass the profile reader's own check (paths inside the folder, the digest the record names, a `SKILL.md` that names the skill) and replaces the folder whole; `skill/remove` `{"name"}`. They need `overlay.write`; the review of what was fetched, and the pin, stay the app's.
- `overlay/needs` `{"name"}` -> `{"items": [{"key", "state": "applied"|"off", "how", "offer"?, "runs"?}]}`: what the installed overlay needs of this computer, as a session would find it (the files must be those its record's digest names), then each dependency file and setup as prepared or not (`runs`: what preparing runs for it) and each command `scripts` names as exposable or not. It starts the programs it asks the version of, so a key needs `overlay.read` and `store.programs`.
- `overlay/prepare` `{"name"}` -> `{"phase": "install"|"sync"|null, "steps": [..]}`: prepares the installed overlay as `aos::preparation` orders it: the state directory emptied (first time), each dependency file installed into a new environment (`python -m venv` with the Python the overlay's `runtime.python` asks for, then `pip install -r`, hash-checked with `--require-hashes --no-deps --only-binary=:all:` when the file is a lock; `npm ci` or `npm install` from copies of `package.json` and its lock alone) that replaces the old one only when it is built, the setup run with `AOS_SETUP_PHASE`, the overlay's root, name and state in its environment, its own process group and the time limit it names (default 300 s), then the record. A step that fails is `-32002` naming it with the end of what it printed. The person's own package-manager settings (`PIP_*`, `.npmrc` of the user) apply. A key needs `overlay.write` and `store.programs`. `overlay/remove` runs setup as `remove` first and deletes the environments, state and record. `store/read_ahead`'s `contents` gain `prepares`, the lines of what installing the entry would run.
- `host/which` `{"names": [..]}` (at most 32, each 1 to 64 of letters, digits, `.`, `_`, `+`, `-`) -> `{"found": {name: path|null}}`: the first runnable file of that name in an absolute directory of the host's `PATH` (Windows: with a `PATHEXT` extension); it starts nothing. The folders of the runtimes the Store installed (Node, Python), when their copy is as it was installed, are searched after `PATH`, so the person's own copy comes first. Needs `store.read`.
- `store/contents` `{"label"}` -> `{"document": text|null}` and `store/contents_put` `{"label", "document"}`: the JSON object the app keeps for a catalog (what each entry provides), under the host's `cache/contents/`, one folder for the person and one for each key, so a key never reads another's; a document is at most 8 MiB, a folder holds 256. Needs `overlay.read`.
- `store/read_ahead` `{"os"?, "entries": [{"id", "kind": "overlay"|"skill", "name", "repo", "ref", "path"?}]}` (at most 32) -> `{"entries": {id: {"contents": {skills, rules, context, knowledge, hooks, env_scripts, setup, mcp_servers, bin, requirements, generated, manifest?, digest, issues}} | {"problem"}}}`: what each entry provides, read here so a page asks once for a batch: an overlay is fetched as `overlay/check` fetches it but under that same isolation, read and discarded (one at a time for a name), a skill as `skill/fetch` fetches it; nothing is installed or run, and an entry that cannot be read is noted against it. Needs `overlay.write`.
- `catalog/fetch` `{"label", "repo", "ref", "path"?}` -> `{"commit"}`; `catalog/read` `{"label", "commit" (whole id), "paths", "maxBytes"?, "list"?}` -> `{"files":
  {path: {"size", "data"?: base64} | null}, "directories"?}` reads regular files at that commit by their literal paths, with no data past `maxBytes`
  (at most 8 MiB) and null for a path that is not a regular file or not a relative path; `list`, a directory's path (`""` the root), adds
  `directories`, the names of its subdirectories (a link or submodule is none) or null when it is no directory, which is how a store reads a directory of overlays as a catalog; `catalog/remove` `{"label"}`.
- **Git** runs as this user from an absolute directory of `PATH` with every `GIT_` variable of the host's removed, `protocol.allow=never` and
  only `https`, `http`, `ssh`, `git` and `file` allowed, no hooks, no fsmonitor, no prompts, 5 minutes at most, and a fetch ended once its
  cache holds 256 MiB. A source on this computer (`file://` or a path) is named by a key only with the `folders` grant, under a working root of an
  entry the key may use (not the home), never in the host's own folders (canoa.cfg's, the overlays', the home it keeps state in) and
  only a repository whose `.git` is a folder; the owner names any. A source on a network share is a place like any other: the owner names it as the file explorer opens it, and a key needs a working root that holds it.
  Git runs from the overlays folder with `GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES`
  set to it. At most 64 fetched caches, and 1 GiB in the overlays folder; the history fetched for a check whose plan expired with
  nothing installed is removed. A fetch locks its own overlay or catalog only. A failure says git's first 300 characters, controls made spaces.
- Skills fetched from elsewhere are not part of this: a page gets none, and an overlay that names one reports it as not delivered.

**The ledger** is the list of the person's sessions, kept by the host in its home (`control/ledger.json`, written whole, atomically)
as opaque JSON objects the host never reads into: it keeps an `id`, the record, who put it and when.

- Every record has an **owner**: `"local"` for the app, else the peer's id. A peer sees and changes only the records it owns, and
  `put` makes it the owner whatever the request says; the owner (the app) sees and changes all.
- **Quotas.** `max_records` and 64 MiB of records are each owner's own (the app's, and each key's), the whole ledger holds 256 MiB, and the last 64 MiB are the app's alone, so a full ledger never refuses the app. A bad `ledger.json` is moved to `ledger.json.bad-<ms>` and an empty ledger begins (logged at `warn`); the file is written, synced and renamed, and `control/` and the file are `0700`/`0600` on Unix. A key's connection silent for 15 minutes is ended.
- `ledger/list` returns the caller's own records only; the owner (the app) asks for every owner's with `"all": true`, which a key's request cannot do. The app reads only its own, so nothing a key put is ever taken for a session. `{"after"?: id, "limit"?: 1..=500, default 100}` -> `{"records": [{"id", "updated", "owner", "record"}], "next"?: id}`,
  newest `updated` first; `next` is the `after` of the following page.
- `ledger/put` `{"id": text of 1 to 128 characters with no control character, "record": object}` -> `{"id", "updated"}`; replaces the
  record of that id (a peer cannot replace another owner's: `-32001`); `updated` is the host's clock, in milliseconds. Refused
  `-32002` past `max_record_bytes`, or past `max_records` for a new id.
- `peer/forget` `{}` -> `{"removed": bool}`: a paired key takes its own pairing out of the trust store, the real revoke (its links end within about a second). It needs no grant, and the owner's link, which holds no key, is refused.
- `ledger/remove` `{"id"}` -> `{"removed": bool}`; `ledger/clear` `{}` -> `{"removed": n}`, the caller's own records.
- A request over 16 MiB, or not JSON, ends the connection; `-32700` first when it can be answered.
